## Jam London 2019

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2019/11/02/jam-2019/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*238 words | Summarized in 6.0s*

Here are 2-3 concise paragraphs summarizing the key points, decisions, and implications of the article:

The articles presents talks from various speakers at Jam London 2019, focusing on topics such as building stories, trust with research teams, agile methodologies, scaling globally, failure, and product development. Chelsea Bullock's talk "Building trust with research teams" emphasizes the importance of mutual trust in team dynamics, and suggests that trusting your research partners is key to moving forward together. Jamie Nicholson's talk "How research inspires new products" stresses the need for early research and user testing to understand people's needs and desires.

The talks also touch on the importance of failure and learning from it. Maryam Mazraei's talk "Failure" highlights the dangers of survivor bias and the importance of analyzing common components of failure to spot patterns. Susana Lopes' talk "You didn’t fail, your product did" emphasizes the need to separate self-worth from product-worth, and suggests that creating product success metrics and personal success metrics can help with emotional resilience.

The articles also highlight the challenges of scaling globally and the importance of adapting to different cultures and user behaviors. Kaarel Kuddu's talk "How to scale globally" discusses the need for experimentation and data-driven decision-making when launching products in new markets. Sabrina Rzepka's talk "Keep pace, master product at BMW (100 years old)" emphasizes the importance of focusing on the essentials and making a vision visible to gain speed with a co-pilot.

## The way of the product manager is death

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2022/01/13/way-of-the-product-manager-death/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*210 words | Summarized in 4.5s*

Here are 2-3 concise paragraphs summarizing the article:

The author draws inspiration from Yamamoto Tsunetomo's book "Hagakure", a collection of commentaries on bushido, the way of the samurai. The book emphasizes the importance of accepting one's own mortality and being mindful of one's actions in the present moment. The author shares various maxims from the book that have helped them navigate challenges, including "Deliberate lightly when deciding on weighty matters" and "Be meticulous when deciding on affairs of minor importance".

The article also touches on the concept of being a generalist, not requiring particular talent to take responsibility for resolving problems. The author suggests taking time to think about one's decisions, subduing self-centered thoughts, and seeking guidance from others with insight. This approach is likened to an enormous tree with many roots, as opposed to a small tree precariously placed in the ground.

The article concludes by discussing various aspects of product management through the lens of bushido principles. It emphasizes the importance of being organized, prepared, and mindful of one's actions, including giving and receiving feedback, planning ahead, and approaching meetings with consideration for others. The author also reflects on the pursuit of perfection as a lifelong quest, highlighting the value of self-reflection and continuous learning in achieving success.

## Blogging and working in the open

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2020/01/25/blogging-working-open/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*208 words | Summarized in 6.8s*

Here are 3 concise paragraphs summarizing the article:

Blogging and working in the open is a crucial aspect of digital transformation in government. Blogging helps to "open up government," form a dialogue with the audience, and share what's learned. By putting experiences out there, people can empathize or respond, developing the transformation project as a whole. The goal of openness is to facilitate collaboration, speed, flexibility, momentum, testing and rapid prototyping, leverage, and participation.

Working in the open causes individuals to be honest about their work, making it easier to collaborate with smart communities of peers. This leads to better outcomes, such as increased agility, participation, and momentum. Blogging is a powerful tool for working in the open, allowing individuals to share knowledge and passion while getting more people involved in agile, user-centered transformation.

To get started with blogging, one can add a "Blog" column to their kanban board, start with weeknotes, or use platforms like WordPress, Ghost, or Medium. There are also resources available for those who want to create a website using static site generators like Jekyll or GitHub Pages. With the right mindset and tools, anyone can start blogging and working in the open, leading to greater collaboration, efficiency, and success in digital transformation projects.

## The Asynchronous Communication

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2020/07/31/the-asynchronous-communication/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*207 words | Summarized in 5.5s*

Here is a summary of the article in 2-3 concise paragraphs:

The author and their team at GOV.UK Pay attempted to test asynchronous communication for a week, with mixed results. The goal was to facilitate distributed working and improve collaboration without relying on meetings and emails as the primary means of information exchange. While some benefits were observed, such as more time in the morning for processing emails and planning goals, others were noted, including difficulties with context-switching and distractions.

The team encountered challenges, including a busy Monday with multiple meetings, which made it difficult to focus. They also found that asynchronous sprint planning felt clunky and that communication principles, such as assuming good intent or taking time to explain oneself, were not in place. By the middle of the week, the author felt overwhelmed by their inbox.

The team plans to implement changes for future experiments, including being more strict with caring for concentration levels and setting do-not-disturb periods on Slack. They also aim to use Pomodoro techniques to break out of inbox- and Slack-surfing. The experiment provided valuable insights into the effectiveness of asynchronous communication in a work environment and highlighted the need for clear communication principles and strategies to mitigate distractions and improve productivity.

## How product teams are using prototyping in the public sector

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2023/12/04/how-product-teams-are-using-prototyping-in-the-public-sector/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*207 words | Summarized in 4.7s*

Here are 2-3 concise paragraphs summarizing the article:

Product teams in the public sector are using prototyping as a practice to inform their product design and development models. The research, conducted at a Product for the People session in Manchester, revealed that most teams (80%) use Figma, with 70% also using Miro or Mural. Designers were involved in prototyping, but only half the teams got developers involved, suggesting it's not dependent on the tools used.

Teams are using various methods to prototype, including low-fidelity paper prototypes, digital tools like Figma and Prototype Kit, and even browser developer tools. Paper prototyping is particularly useful for engaging non-digital colleagues from Policy and Communications departments, while technical prototypes can help test out complex services. Some teams also use Lean techniques like Wizard of Oz and Concierge to prototype the operations layer of a service.

The research highlights the importance of investing in tools and training to support prototyping practices. The author suggests that Figma could be used as an officially supported component library for GOV.UK, and that integrating technical prototypes earlier in the development process can lead to quicker beta phases. Additionally, better training, pay, and tools are needed to bridge the gap between public and private sectors' prototyping capabilities.

## The Quiet

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2019/11/09/the-quiet/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*204 words | Summarized in 5.2s*

Here are 2-3 concise paragraphs summarizing the key points, decisions, and implications:

The author reflects on their personal life and work during a pre-election period in the UK. They mention spending less time cycling due to chilly mornings, but after encouragement from their partner, they got back on their bike and found it helped them gain clarity and confidence.

In the context of product management, strategy credits and tax are discussed. Strategy credits refer to decisions that make a company look good relative to others who face more significant trade-offs. A strategy tax is anything that makes a product less likely to succeed but is included to further larger corporate goals. These concepts can help individuals step back and consider how their decisions contribute to the overall direction of their work.

The author also touches on the importance of exploration, discovery, and uncertain futures in design practice. They reference Will Myddleton's writing on running discoveries and creating a safe space for discovery teams. The goal is not to resolve a system but to model possibilities that system interventions might provoke. The author emphasizes the need for advocates for discovery at a senior level and for stopping pressure on discovery teams to validate pre-existing decisions.

## Slow travel

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2022/10/01/slow-travel/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*199 words | Summarized in 3.7s*

Here is a summary of the article in 2-3 concise paragraphs:

Slow travel is an approach to traveling that involves taking routes less traveled, avoiding main roads and airplanes, and making decisions on the go. This concept shares similarities with the slow food movement, which promotes buying local produce and trying new flavors to reduce waste and improve sustainability. Slow travel aims to provide a more immersive experience.

The idea of slow travel has gained momentum in recent years, thanks to initiatives such as Slow Travel UK, Welcome to My Garden, and other online movements and tools. These platforms offer resources and support for individuals who want to explore the world at their own pace, often sleeping in people's gardens or camping along quiet paths. For many people, slow travel provides a welcome distraction from economic and political despair.

However, the article also raises concerns about homelessness and rural homelessness being on the rise. The availability of tools for vagrancy coexists with the conditions that force people onto the streets. Despite this, there is hope in the community's response to challenges such as Homes for Ukraine, which showed the population's capacity to care for those suddenly uprooted by circumstance.

## Universal interfaces and Semantic Agents

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2024/01/22/universal-interfaces-and-semantic-agents/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*198 words | Summarized in 4.8s*

Here are 3 concise paragraphs summarizing the article:

The Rabbit R1 is an AI-powered personal assistant that can perform tasks on behalf of users, such as creating playlists or planning holidays. It uses a "Large Action Model" to understand website and app interactions and translates user requests into actions. However, its implementation has raised concerns about enabling permissions for actions, prompt engineering, and the potential limitations of its capabilities.

The device's focus on privacy means that users need to grant verified access to their chosen apps and services through another device. This creates a barrier to using the Rabbit R1 as a smartphone companion, rather than a replacement. Additionally, the device may struggle with common tasks due to the complexity of integrating with various platforms and services.

The article draws parallels between the Rabbit R1 and Tim Berners-Lee's 2001 concept of the Semantic Web, which aims to make applications meaningful to machines and other applications. However, the current state of platform integration and third-party application limitations may hinder the Rabbit R1's ability to deliver value to users. The device's success depends on its ability to overcome these hurdles and convince platforms to open up access to their APIs.

## Design systems are a folk craft

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2022/12/15/design-systems-folk-craft/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*197 words | Summarized in 3.8s*

Here are 2-3 concise paragraphs summarizing the article:

Design systems are a practical and efficient way to approach user experience design, particularly in early-stage startups. Instead of trying to create a unique and magical user experience, designers should focus on adopting a design system that provides a foundation for common problems, validated through robust research. This approach allows designers to build on existing solutions rather than starting from scratch.

A design system is a collection of reusable components, patterns, and guidelines that can be assembled together to build various applications. It's a digital folk craft, meaning it's a common, ordinary solution to everyday problems that has been refined over time through research and community feedback. By adopting a design system, designers can tap into the expertise of others and focus on iterating and improving existing solutions rather than trying to create something entirely new.

By recognizing the value of design systems, designers can avoid wasting time on tedious tasks like crafting perfect UI components or hover states. Instead, they can trust their designers to combine reusable solutions in a way that works for users, freeing up time and resources to focus on more important problems and growth.

## What does it mean to 'personalise' government?

*195 words | Summarized in 5.6s*

Here are 2-3 concise paragraphs summarizing the article:

The concept of "personalisation" in government refers to tailoring services and content to meet individual users' needs. A team at GOV.UK explored this idea, asking themselves what it would mean to personalise the experience of interacting with government. They conducted desk research on how private-sector companies like Facebook, Amazon, and Google use personalisation, and interviewed 8 international governments to gather insights.

The team identified seven criteria for evaluating potential solutions: assumed solution, user motivation, personal data needed, trust in government, access method, required skills, and data ownership. They also explored speculative designs to facilitate conversations across the organisation about what could be done for users. The goal is not to increase engagement but to enable greater success by making services simpler, clearer, and faster.

The team will now prototype solutions to meet user needs, using GOV.UK's feedback channels and previous research as a starting point. They aim to understand users' behaviors, approaches to interacting with government online, and how it fits into their lives. The ultimate goal is to make completing whole services simpler, clearer, and faster, without requiring users to have in-depth knowledge of government services.

## Slow discovery, fast discovery

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2022/10/31/slow-discovery-fast-discovery/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*194 words | Summarized in 3.8s*

Here are 3 concise paragraphs summarizing the article:

The author is experiencing difficulties with discovering people and conversations on Mastodon, a platform they transitioned to from Twitter. They find that the lack of discoverability forces them to create multiple accounts or personas, which feels similar to the old forum model but without the separation between communities. This limits their ability to engage with specific topics.

The author attributes this problem to Mastodon's decentralized nature, which makes it harder to index and search content. In contrast, Twitter's centralized approach allows for fast discovery through indexing and searching. The author believes that Mastodon's slower discoverability is a deliberate design choice, which creates friction and limits their ability to find interesting people and topics.

The author notes that the shift from fast discovery to slow discovery has implications beyond just Mastodon. It can lead to a more active and engaged user experience, where users take control of their own discovery process rather than relying on algorithms or centralized services. They use the example of RSS subscriptions, which require effort and browsing to discover new content, as an alternative model that allows for slower and more meaningful discovery.

The club allows members to choose what food to bring and enjoy the spontaneity of not choosing their own meal.

The author suggests taking the Lunch Club idea further by providing kitchen space for collaboration, allowing staff to cook together. However, most offices were designed as workplaces rather than culture spaces, making it challenging to create such an environment. ## Must-haves when starting or joining a product team

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2020/08/15/essentials-for-starting-and-joining-product-teams/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*191 words | Summarized in 3.9s*

Here is a summary of the article in 2-3 concise paragraphs:

When starting or joining a product team, there are several key principles to keep in mind. The foundational principle is to ensure that the team has a clear view of what success looks like and how they will achieve it. This involves defining the product or service, identifying its purpose and goals, and understanding the pain points it solves for users.

To work effectively in a team, establish shared principles and ways of working early on, and make sure roles and responsibilities are clear to everyone. Regular check-ins with the delivery manager can also help ensure that planning is effective. Additionally, creating psychological safety and building trust by being open about mistakes and explaining how they will be improved can help foster a positive team culture.

When it comes to decision-making, prioritize based on universal criteria such as reach, impact, confidence, effort, and urgency. Avoid making big decisions based on limited research or testing studies, and instead rely on evidence and testing to inform your decisions. It's also important to present risks and show working to gain buy-in from stakeholders.

## The problems with making answers in data more findable

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2024/05/26/the-problems-with-making-answers-in-data-more-findable/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*191 words | Summarized in 5.5s*

Here are 2-3 concise paragraphs summarizing the article:

The author discusses the challenges of making answers in data more findable, citing four problems: (1) finding datasets as a researcher or analyst is difficult, (2) not all users have the coding expertise needed to get answers from data, (3) mechanisms for sharing code are not always suited to or safe for analysis of government or healthcare data, and (4) most people find answers through Google.

The author proposes hypothetical solutions to these problems. To address problem 1, they suggest publishing questions or answers alongside statistical releases to make them findable on the web. For problem 2, a platform for sharing code to run analyses on datasets could help bridge the programming skills gap. Problem 3 can be addressed by creating a community of researchers and data scientists working on government or healthcare data, where context-specific sharing of code and answers can occur.

The author concludes that improving data discoverability is not enough; people need access to previous analyses to solve complex problems like the housing crisis or pandemic. They suggest designing data services around user behavior, rather than building yet another catalogue.

## Visual design fundamentals

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2021/06/24/visual-design-fundamentals/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*190 words | Summarized in 4.8s*

Here are 3 concise paragraphs summarizing the article:

The article discusses visual design fundamentals, focusing on the aesthetics of a project and its related materials. Visual design aims to create trust in a project without compromising its content or function. To achieve this, designers must conduct research, understand the client's objectives, and craft an objective that answers the questions: "What are we trying to communicate? Who are we communicating it to?"

The article highlights key concepts such as contrast, Gestalt principles of perception, space, colour, typography, and moodboarding. Contrast is used to convey information and importance, while Gestalt principles help designers create visually appealing compositions. Space is essential for establishing hierarchy, emphasis, and drama, while colour can evoke emotions and reinforce branding messages. Typography is subjective but plays a crucial role in conveying meaning.

The article emphasizes the importance of research, objective-setting, and constraint-definition in visual design. Designers must adapt to different briefs and processes, using techniques like moodboarding and typography to create effective designs. The article concludes by highlighting the value of understanding human perception and psychology in visual design, including concepts such as emergence, reification, multi-stability, and invariance.

## How we use OKRs on GOV.UK

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2019/02/21/how-we-use-okrs-gov-uk/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*185 words | Summarized in 4.8s*

Here is a summary of the article in 2-3 concise paragraphs:

The UK government's website, GOV.UK, uses Objectives and Key Results (OKRs) to set measurable goals for agile teams. The framework provides a light-touch way to define objectives, achieve outcomes, and track progress. OKRs are used by product teams to deliver value to users, with the objective setting direction for the company or programme.

An objective is a bold and qualitative goal that outlines what the company or programme wants to achieve. Key results are tangible and quantitative goals that outline how the team will achieve the objective. The key result should be specific, measurable, achievable, and realistic. OKRs provide a framework for teams to work together and align with senior management.

The use of OKRs on GOV.UK allows product teams to take ownership of their goals and deliver value in interesting ways. Leadership can set ambitious objectives that teams can buy into, and the framework provides space for teams to solve problems creatively. Regular progress reporting helps ensure alignment between product teams and senior management, and allows for adjustments to be made as needed.

## The Nonce Age collapse

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2022/05/19/nonce-age-collapse/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*179 words | Summarized in 3.4s*

Here is a summary of the article in 2-3 concise paragraphs:

The author discusses how humans have exhibited a pattern of behavior throughout history, where new technologies or objects become desirable and status-oriented. This pattern was observed in ancient times, such as when people polished stone axes to make them more tradable and aesthetically pleasing. Similarly, during the Bronze Age, bronze axes were valued not only for their functionality but also for their decorative value.

The author notes that a similar phenomenon is happening with cryptocurrencies and NFTs (non-fungible tokens). Initially, they were seen as useful tools, but over time, they have become primarily status objects. This has led to a "Nonce Age collapse," where the value of these technologies or objects begins to decline.

A recent tweet by David Spinks illustrates this phenomenon, with him stating that he is selling most of his NFTs because he no longer believes in communities centered around owning them having longevity. The author suggests that it's essential to consider the functionality and practicality of a technology or object over its aesthetic value.

## Product teams deliver outcomes, not outputs

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2019/04/27/product-teams-deliver-outcomes-not-outputs/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*174 words | Summarized in 3.8s*

Here are 2-3 concise paragraphs summarizing the article:

Product teams deliver outcomes, not outputs. Outputs refer to tangible tasks or actions a team produces, whereas outcomes are the end results a team aims to achieve. The key distinction is that producing an output is often seen as manufacturing work, while producing an outcome requires domain expertise and experimentation.

To turn "Build this" into "Realise that", leadership teams must share their vision and help product teams understand why a task is important. This can be achieved through discussions like the 5 Whys, which helps to identify the root reason behind a task. By doing so, teams can set outcome-focused objectives that align with the organization's goals.

Unlearning the traditional output-based leadership style requires a shift in mindset for leadership teams. Instead of managing effort and output, they should focus on setting outcome-based objectives that enable product teams to track progress and learn. This approach helps communication about context and priorities, ensuring product teams are clear on what is expected of them to achieve organizational success.

## Don’t just keep the lights on, shine bright

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2025/07/31/dont-just-keep-the-lights-on-shine-bright/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*173 words | Summarized in 4.1s*

Here is a summary of the article in 3 concise paragraphs:

The concept of "shining bright" refers to government services that not only meet minimum requirements but also provide value and meet user needs. Unlike private-sector services, where users have a choice, public sector organizations are often stuck with subpar solutions due to limited options.

To avoid this fate, platform teams working on government-built platforms should think like startups and focus on growing their market share. They must create value for money, track benefits realized, and continuously improve their offerings. Incremental growth is acceptable, but teams should aim to make significant leaps every 1-2 years by solving big problems for users.

Leaders play a crucial role in promoting the principles of Government as a Platform. They must ensure that teams have the necessary resources and funding to deliver value to users. When benefits slow or stagnate, teams should investigate the reasons and identify areas for improvement. By doing so, they can make an impact, drive growth, and ultimately create economic benefits for taxpayers.

The author participated in the London Spring 10K 2022, aiming to complete the 10km in 44:44 and use it as a training block for the Pen Llŷn Ultra 2023. The race was held on June 11, 2022, with an expected time of 4:45/km.

During the race, the author started too fast, achieving a pace of 4:17/km, which ruined their planned negative splits and contributed to not meeting their goal of completing the 10km in under 44 minutes. They struggled with dehydration and fatigue, especially after the first lap, but managed to maintain an average pace of 4:24/km until the seventh kilometre.

The author concluded that the mistake was due to poor pre-race preparation, specifically not hydrating properly before the race. Despite this setback, they finished the 10km in 45:03, 19 seconds off their goal time. The experience taught them valuable lessons about planning ahead for toilets and hydration, as well as the importance of a proper warm-up before racing.

## Making notes, taking actions

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2025/07/17/making-notes-taking-actions/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*169 words | Summarized in 4.0s*

Here are 3 concise paragraphs summarizing the article:

The author, Harsh Browns, set out to automate mundane tasks such as taking meeting notes and summarizing discussions using generative AI. He started by experimenting with pre-existing products like Google Meet transcription and Copilot summarization, but found they were not customizable enough. Therefore, he decided to roll his own transcription and summarization automation.

The author's setup involves recording audio from meetings using MacWhisper, which transcribes the recordings into a time-stamped transcript. He then uses an LM Studio server running on his Mac to generate summaries from these transcripts. The process is surprisingly simple and takes less than a minute to complete.

Harsh Browns justifies his decision to automate these tasks by highlighting the trade-offs. By outsourcing certain tasks, he gains more control over the information that is acted upon and can focus on being a more active listener and contributor in meetings. He also values the sustainability of using his own machine and parameters, which limits who can access the summaries.

The clinic sessions focus on soft skills, product strategy, and tools related to product management. The process is informal, relying on openness and vulnerability within the community. The author takes notes during the sessions to share with others who may find them helpful.

Starting a product clinic can be beneficial for product managers struggling with problems at work. It allows them to get things out of their head rather than suffering in silence. The approach has been recognized as valuable not only for product managers but also for digital practitioners in general, and the author suggests exploring its application beyond the GOV.UK context.

## Improving pace of delivery

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2024/01/26/improving-pace-of-delivery/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*168 words | Summarized in 3.2s*

Here are 2-3 concise paragraphs summarizing the article:

The author, Harsh Browns, discusses the challenge of improving the pace of delivery in software development. He shares a personal anecdote about feeling overwhelmed when starting out in Agile and suggests that a framework for improvement can be applied to both running and software delivery.

The framework is based on Christopher McDougall's book "Born to Run" and involves four stages: "Think Easy, Light, Smooth, and Fast". The first stage involves taking it easy and learning the ins and outs, while the second stage focuses on being light on one's feet, becoming more proactive and efficient. The third stage aims to make things feel smooth, pre-empting bumps in the road and mitigating risks early. The final stage allows for natural improvement as the process becomes effortless.

The author implies that by following this framework, teams can improve their delivery pace over time. He suggests focusing on iterations rather than big-bang releases and descope projects as needed to maintain a smooth flow.

## Estimating software projects: what I’ve learned

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2023/09/13/estimating-software-projects/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*168 words | Summarized in 3.4s*

Here are 2-3 concise paragraphs summarizing the article:

The author shares their approach to estimating software projects, which involves framing estimation as a useful exercise that helps teams plan releases and make decisions. They emphasize that accurate estimation is impossible and instead advocate for using broad brushstrokes to estimate time blocks. The author also highlights the importance of good communication and transparency in estimation.

The article outlines six rules for estimating software projects: (1) focus on process value over output, (2) prioritize communication over accuracy, (3) explain how estimations will be used, (4) acknowledge that there are always undiscovered tasks, (5) revisit estimations regularly to adapt to changes, and (6) avoid using estimates as a punitive measure against individuals.

The author argues that estimating software projects can be valuable when done correctly, but it requires a mindset shift from focusing on individual accuracy to prioritizing process value and communication. By following these rules, teams can create a more collaborative and adaptive estimation process that supports their overall goals.

## Why I stopped writing weeknotes

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2022/08/09/why-i-stopped-writing-weeknotes/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*167 words | Summarized in 3.5s*

Here are 3 concise paragraphs summarizing the article:

Harsh Browns, a former employee of GDS (Government Digital Service), stopped writing weeknotes around 12 months ago. He initially wanted to continue writing about his experiences but felt that doing so could compromise the security and competitiveness of their early-stage startup.

Browns' decision was influenced by the need for openness being "neutered" due to the presence of competitors trying to gather information. He also missed the accountability and conversation-starting opportunities that came with publishing his weeknotes online. As a product manager, he relied on his product sense to make decisions, which was aided by writing weeknotes.

Browns has now started writing weeknotes again after switching jobs to NHS Digital. He is cautious about following GDS' culture but feels the need to share his thoughts and experiences with others. By doing so, he aims to start a conversation and potentially provide alternatives or workarounds for those who face similar challenges in maintaining openness while working in sensitive or competitive environments.

## Too many hands on the tiller

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2025/08/13/too-many-hands-on-the-tiller/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*167 words | Summarized in 2.8s*

Here is a summary of the article in 2-3 concise paragraphs:

The article discusses the importance of having only one "hand on the tiller" when it comes to leadership and decision-making. The author references a Seneca quote that emphasizes the need to know your destination before making decisions, and uses sailing as an analogy.

The author argues that too many people trying to set direction can lead to confusion and poor decision-making, regardless of the size of the organization or team. A captain may listen to their crew's feedback and adjust course accordingly, but having too many people pulling in different directions can cause problems.

In today's world, leadership is more like being an admiral, where you have influence over multiple teams rather than being on every one personally. The author emphasizes the importance of clear direction, clear decisions, and clear coordination, suggesting that leaders need to strike a balance between giving autonomy to their team members and making sure everyone is working towards the same goal.

*167 words | Summarized in 3.3s*

The author, a former product manager at Government Digital Service (GDS), is missing the community and camaraderie they experienced while working there. They now work as a solo product manager in a startup and long for a similar support network to share ideas, resources, and personal experiences.

To address this need, the author aims to start a "product circle" - a group of product managers who meet regularly to discuss their projects, share knowledge, hold each other accountable, and provide mutual support. The idea was inspired by Tobi Ogunsina's concept of "circles for growth," which emphasizes key factors such as peer coaching, shared perspective, and trust-building.

The author invites senior product managers working in early or growth stage startups to join the virtual circle, where they can share their experiences, challenges, and successes on a regular basis. They believe that this community will provide a valuable space for product friends to connect, learn from each other, and grow professionally.

## Reasons to cancel your Pocket subscription

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2022/11/20/reasons-to-cancel-your-pocket-subscription/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*167 words | Summarized in 3.0s*

Here are 3 concise paragraphs summarizing the article:

The author has been a long-time user of Pocket, a reading and writing app that has been a key part of their daily routine for four years. They have used it to store articles, read, highlight passages, and access archived content, which has provided them with several benefits.

However, the author has encountered some limitations in Pocket's functionality. Specifically, they found its search feature to be unreliable, favoring tokens over phrases and failing to parse certain websites, particularly those from the 1990s. Additionally, highlighting passages on iOS was sometimes disabled due to website-specific issues.

In response to these frustrations, the author discovered an alternative app called Reader, which is being developed by Readwise. After trying out Reader's private beta, they found it to be a superior product that offers a better search experience, handles multiple document types, and integrates with highlight tagging and reviewing features. As a result, the author has cancelled their Pocket subscription and switched to Reader.

## A team for thinking big and starting small

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2022/11/09/a-team-for-thinking-big-and-starting-small/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*167 words | Summarized in 3.1s*

Here is a summary of the article in 2-3 concise paragraphs:

A team for "thinking big and starting small" aims to bring captivating visions to life by creating a first small step on the path. This model is not new, but it's essential to make the team responsible for this initial step, which requires building, owning, sustaining, and proving the strategy.

To succeed, the team needs a deep understanding of the organization, its people, and its mission. This approach is about sustaining innovation, using hard-won knowledge, passion, and momentum as fuel. It's not about creating an innovation lab or letting one team have grand ideas without accountability.

The goal is to create a tangible, inspiring vision that encourages experimentation and testing of ideas against reality. Once the route seems more sure, the team can start moving in that direction, bringing others along and achieving something they can be proud of. This approach requires knowledge, passion, time, and commitment, but it's essential for starting change in an organization.

## How far we’ve come

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2024/02/10/how-far-weve-come/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*166 words | Summarized in 4.5s*

Here are 3 concise paragraphs summarizing the article:

The author is reflecting on what it would mean to lose the GOV.‌UK Design System, a framework that brings consistency and unity to digital services across government departments. The system has been instrumental in achieving a "BBC-esque" online presence, with consistent templates, universal top bars, and high-quality UI/design.

The GOV.‌UK Design System is crucial for sustaining a widely distributed ecosystem of digital services, allowing them to produce simple, consistent, and welcoming experiences for users. It enables innovation at the edges of the ecosystem to feed back into the center, standardizing improvements and driving value. Without it, government services would likely fall behind in terms of user experience.

The author notes that the GOV.‌UK Design System needs to continue evolving and adapting to changing user behavior and digital trends. It must remain a key component in making hundreds of websites and digital services feel like one thing, ensuring that the user experience remains simple, consistent, and welcoming for everyone.

## Certainty, probability, risk

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2024/04/11/certainty-probability-risk/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*165 words | Summarized in 3.3s*

Here are 2-3 concise paragraphs summarizing the article:

The article discusses the importance of switching one's mindset from "certainty" to "probability" in decision-making. Certainty can paralyze action, while probability allows for a more agile approach by acknowledging that outcomes may vary. This mindset shift is particularly relevant in high-stakes contexts such as international hunts or complex problem-solving.

The article references the movie Zero Dark Thirty, where a team discusses the location of Osama Bin Laden, initially focusing on certainty rather than probability. However, after Maya presents a high-probability scenario, the group shifts their approach to include options and planning for different outcomes. This example illustrates how talking about probability can help teams make more informed decisions.

The article concludes by emphasizing the importance of being agile in decision-making by breaking down big goals into smaller, manageable chunks, and using options to reduce risk. By acknowledging uncertainty and working towards improving probabilities, individuals and teams can increase their chances of success while still taking calculated risks.

## Agentic AI needs UCD smarts

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2025/02/07/agentic-ai-needs-ucd-smarts/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*163 words | Summarized in 3.2s*

Here is a summary of the article in 3 concise paragraphs:

Agentic AI, which interacts with the real world through actions, requires "User-Centered Design" (UCD) smarts to develop reliable and safe models. A critical step in LAM (Large Action Model) development is evaluation, which assesses reliability, robustness, and safety before deployment. This involves rigorous testing and adaptation of UCD methods for computer-computer interaction.

The online evaluation stage is considered the most critical part of LAM development, as it introduces new risks due to the model's ability to directly affect its environment through actions. Thorough evaluation processes are essential to ensure that both the LAM and its accompanying agent make reliable decisions while minimizing potential risks.

As agentic AI gains prominence, ethical concerns arise regarding accountability, transparency, and fairness. Product and design professionals with experience in government or regulatory environments may be well-suited to join AI teams, as they will need to understand not only agents as users but also the users of agents.

## Patterns for collaborative blogging

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2022/10/04/patterns-for-collaborative-blogging/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*162 words | Summarized in 3.3s*

Here are 3 concise paragraphs summarizing the article:

Tom Critchlow discusses the idea of collaborative blogging, aiming to make blog posts a more interactive and co-created experience. He identifies several patterns for collaborative blogging, including content coaching, pair writing, workshopping, missives, and threading.

These patterns facilitate collaboration among writers, allowing them to share ideas, provide feedback, and build on each other's work. Content coaching involves a writer working with a coach or mentor to refine their ideas. Pair writing involves two people working together on a single blog post. Workshopping involves sharing a piece of writing with a group and receiving feedback.

Threading is another pattern that enables writers to create a conversation across multiple posts, where one writer inspires another to write a response. The goal of collaborative blogging is to reduce the feeling of solitary blogging by leveraging collaboration and discussion. Critchlow invites readers to share their own patterns for collaborative blogging, which he will update as new ideas emerge.

## The free-range future of work

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2020/11/02/free-range-future-of-work/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*162 words | Summarized in 3.8s*

Here are 2-3 concise paragraphs summarizing the article:

The COVID-19 pandemic has forced organizations to adopt remote working, known as "free-range working," which allows workers to spread their workforce geographically and temporally. This shift has created a new normal for many employees, who must adapt to new ways of working and collaborating without being physically present.

To facilitate free-range working, managers and team members are encouraged to build their own New Normal by creating a collaborative environment that fosters serendipity, innovation, and inclusive communication. Strategies include setting up tea breaks channels, sharing work-in-progress with colleagues, and creating opportunities for spontaneous socializing. Additionally, considering the role-specific needs of employees, revitalizing communities of practice, and maintaining boundaries between work and personal life are crucial.

Effective free-range working requires intentional effort to create a supportive and inclusive environment that balances individual needs with team goals. By prioritizing energy management, minimizing distractions during meetings, and cultivating diverse voices, teams can thrive in this new remote landscape.

## The tech hype-cycle is spinning ever faster

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2023/10/22/the-tech-hype-cycle-is-spinning-ever-faster/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*158 words | Summarized in 2.8s*

Here are 2-3 concise paragraphs summarizing the article:

The tech hype-cycle is spinning ever faster, with increased pressure for breakthroughs to happen more quickly and for the next big thing to appear. This has led to a narrower view of where progress is most important, relying on moonshot ambitions of billionaires and charitable foundations.

A steady-state model of innovation would alter the dynamics of the hype-cycle by creating useful friction, allowing time to evaluate the ethical implications of technologies or products in more detail. This approach would enable research and development teams to focus on demonstrating widely-applicable, socially useful research over a longer period.

This shift would require significant changes, including slowing down the pace of innovation and focusing on creating meaningful experiences rather than just announcing new technologies. However, this deceleration could be challenging for managers to communicate upwards to leaders and executives, potentially requiring a deeper understanding (or lack thereof) among the workforce regarding hyped technologies.

---

## Handwritten vs typed notes

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2023/10/16/handwritten-vs-typed-notes/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*158 words | Summarized in 3.0s*

Here is a summary of the article in 3 concise paragraphs:

Research suggests that handwriting notes can be more effective than typing them, particularly in improving recall and understanding of complex ideas. This is because writing down information engages multiple brain systems, including motor and sensory systems, which can aid memory retention.

However, the author of the article questions whether handwriting notes are inherently superior to typing. They argue that students who take handwritten notes consistently outperform those who type their notes due to the compression of ideas during the writing process, but suggest that this same idea compression can also be achieved through typing.

The key takeaway is that the effectiveness of note-taking method depends on how well one engages with the material and processes it mentally. While handwriting may have an advantage in terms of recall and comprehension, typing notes can still be effective if done thoughtfully and with a focus on summarizing and compressing ideas.

## Jam together

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2023/09/18/jam-together/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*156 words | Summarized in 2.5s*

Here are 3 concise paragraphs summarizing the article:

Good product teams can be compared to jazz bands, where collaboration and improvisation are key. To achieve this, teams need to have a deep understanding of each other's strengths and weaknesses, as well as a strong foundation in technical skills and creativity.

To create an environment that fosters collaboration and improvisation, teams must prioritize psychological safety and take steps to fail safely. This can be achieved by creating spaces for experimentation and failure, such as practicing work processes on silly tasks or taking breaks from work to build shared identity.

Product managers play a crucial role in creating and maintaining this environment. They need to dedicate time to building collaboration and communication skills within their team, and be willing to adapt and learn alongside their colleagues. By doing so, they can create teams that are able to work together seamlessly and deliver high-quality products through pure collaborative improvisation.

## How to setup Jekyll with Tailwind CSS

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2021/12/18/how-to-setup-jekyll-with-tailwind-css/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*155 words | Summarized in 3.7s*

Here are 3 concise paragraphs summarizing the article:

To set up Jekyll with Tailwind CSS, the author followed a tutorial by Marcin Doliwa and adapted it for macOS Monterey. The prerequisites include installing Xcode, Homebrew, rbenv, nvm, Jekyll, and various packages using npm.

The steps to set up Jekyll with Tailwind CSS involve creating a new blank Jekyll website, installing required packages, setting up PostCSS, and configuring the Tailwind CSS config file. Specifically, this includes creating a Gemfile, adding the jekyll-postcss gem, running bundle install, adding plugins to the _config.yml file, creating a postcss.config.js file, generating an empty Tailwind config file with npx tailwindcss init, importing Tailwind stylesheets in the main.scss file, and finally running Jekyll.

Once set up, Jekyll can be run using jekyll serve, and the site will be accessible at localhost:4000. The author successfully completed these steps after a few hours of troubleshooting, resulting in a working Jekyll site with Tailwind CSS.

## Universal Interface? No, thank you

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2023/11/14/universal-interface-no-thank-you/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*153 words | Summarized in 3.3s*

Here are 2-3 concise paragraphs summarizing the article:

The concept of a "Universal Interface" introduced by Ben Thompson in a recent Stratechery post refers to an AI system that can automatically select and use different plug-ins or modules based on user intent, without requiring manual selection. However, author Harsh Browns disagrees with this idea, citing the potential loss of agency and transparency.

Browns argues that such an interface would strip away the benefits of browsing the web, which is a key aspect of the internet's functionality. He also notes that language itself is complex and context-dependent, making it impossible for AI to fully understand user intent without human oversight.

Instead, Browns suggests that AI should be designed as "team-mates" or NPCs (non-player characters) that assist humans in their tasks, rather than replacing them entirely. This approach would allow for a balance between convenience and transparency, while preserving the unique qualities of human experience.

## Rhetoric: leading teams with memes

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2020/11/07/rhetoric/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*148 words | Summarized in 2.9s*

Here is a summary of the article in 3 concise paragraphs:

The author discusses how GDS's original motto "Trust, Users, Delivery" was used to communicate concisely and effectively. The phrase was memorable and understandable because it tapped into Aristotle's rhetorical triangle of appeal: pathos (emotional impact), logos (logical reasoning), and ethos (appeal to authority).

Each element of the phrase addressed a specific aspect of GDS's digital transformation effort. Pathos appealed to users' emotions, while logos presented logical reasons for the strategy, citing agile methods and disruptive innovation. Ethos appealed to trust by framing the effort as something done for the benefit of the country.

The use of this phrase was effective because it provided both a "why" (making change happen for the country) and a "how" (using methods that work). The short, memorable phrase made sense and worked well in tense moments, making it an effective communication tool.

## Notes on areas of responsibility

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2020/09/26/notes-on-areas-of-responsibility/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*146 words | Summarized in 3.0s*

Here are 2-3 concise paragraphs summarizing the article:

The concept of "Areas of Responsibility" (AoRs) is a system used by Apple to assign tasks and responsibilities to individuals, ensuring accountability and efficiency. By designating specific areas to each person, it allows for clear answers when questions arise about who is responsible for what.

This approach extends down the ranks, with internal Applespeak using the term "DRI" (directly responsible individual) to identify those in charge of a particular area. The system encourages transparency and knowledge sharing, allowing people to develop deep expertise and become best equipped to tackle problems when others take over.

To implement this system, product managers can create a spreadsheet with areas as rows and owners as columns, marking the level of knowledge each person has about an area (0-5). This helps identify opportunities for knowledge transfer and ensures that responsibilities are distributed evenly.

## Alpha is discovery

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2022/04/09/alpha-is-discovery/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*144 words | Summarized in 3.8s*

Here are 3 concise paragraphs summarizing the article:

The traditional discovery and alpha stages are not effective for teams, as they create uncertainty and hinder progress. Instead, the author proposes viewing discovery and alpha as a loop, where learnings from exploring solutions feed back into understanding of the problem. This approach optimizes for learning velocity over delivery velocity.

To create this loop, teams should focus on sketching ideas quickly and validating them to check understanding. Sketches have fleeting value but allow for rapid ideation and refinement. They can take many forms, not just UI designs, and are a valuable tool for exploring and refining ideas.

By adopting this approach, teams can speed up learning and reduce uncertainty by becoming comfortable with showing scrappy ideas. This is at the heart of lean product development, where thinking and making become one activity, improving with each iteration.

## So, you want to build AI for professionals

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2025/04/07/so-you-want-to-build-ai-for-professionals/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*143 words | Summarized in 3.7s*

Here are 2-3 concise paragraphs summarizing the article:

Building AI for professionals requires consideration of their unique needs and constraints. Professionals often have an ethical dimension to their work, with codes of conduct and professional standards guiding their actions. For instance, town planners use AI in planning processes, which must present its data and methodology transparently.

Trust in AI is heavily dependent on the user's perception of its ability to make informed decisions. Design patterns can be used to show confidence, change models, and share information that shaped the output. However, downstream processes such as regulatory compliance also need to be considered.

Designing with these constraints in mind can lead to a productive realism within them. By understanding the ethical dimensions of professions and incorporating explainability and transparency into AI tools, developers can create more trustworthy systems that meet the needs of professionals.

## For Margaret Calvert fans

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2025/01/25/for-fans-of-margaret-calvert/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*142 words | Summarized in 2.5s*

Here is a summary of the article in 2-3 concise paragraphs:

The author has been taking photographs of signs using Rail Alphabet, a typeface designed by Jock Kinneir and Margaret Calvert for signage on the British Rail network. The photos aim to serve as a record of the single identity used across a fragmented national rail network.

Rail Alphabet was introduced in 1965 and replaced Transport, which was originally designed for road signage. It remained in use until sectorisation broke up the national rail network in the late 1980s. The author's photographs are being posted on typo.social/@RailAlphabet, a Mastodon instance dedicated to typography enthusiasts.

The project aims to preserve the unique visual identity of Rail Alphabet before it may eventually disappear. By documenting these signs, the author hopes to create a record of the single brand used across different stations and networks.

## Work is gambling, gambling is zen

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2022/10/22/work-is-gambling-is-zen/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*141 words | Summarized in 2.6s*

Here are 3 concise paragraphs summarizing the article:

The author compares work to gambling, and vice versa, as expressed in Walter Benjamin's writing on gambling. According to Benjamin, a gambler's luck is not about making rational decisions, but rather being sensitive to their intuition and instinct.

This idea is echoed by Alan Watts' concept of "peripheral vision," which suggests that our most important decisions often rely on intuition rather than analysis. The author notes that in the internet era, many knowledge work situations require relying on intuition or instinct, as excessive analysis can lead to "analysis paralysis."

The author reflects on their own job as a facilitator of decision-making, where they must rely on this peripheral vision and intuition to navigate different contexts and actors. They acknowledge that this approach requires taking risks and "gambling" in order to make effective decisions.

On October 25, 1846, a wooden ship called the Pestonjee Bomanjee left the coast of Kent, bound for Van Diemen's Land (now Tasmania), carrying James Messer, the fourth-great grandfather of the author. Messer was a butcher from Hertford who had been convicted twice for selling stolen sheep.

The ship's surgeon described Messer in the ship's records, which has provided valuable information about his appearance. According to the description, Messer would have looked like the author's father, suggesting a physical similarity between the two.

This event marks an important part of the author's family history, and they have been trying to learn more about their ancestor's life through research and ancestral memories. The discovery of the ship's surgeon's description has provided new insights into Messer's appearance and identity.

## Best, not first

*Source: https://visitmy.website/2023/10/24/best-not-first/?utm_campaign=rss&utm_source=rss*
*135 words | Summarized in 2.4s*

Here are 2-3 concise paragraphs summarizing the article:

Apple's approach to AI is a good example of prioritizing utility over being first. While Google and Microsoft have led in developing generative AI, Apple has focused on integrating AI into its autocorrect feature, which is used by billions of people daily.

This approach aligns with the "Best, not first" philosophy, suggesting that public services should prioritize meaningful integration over flashy announcements. Unlike private companies driven by competition, public services don't need to rush to be the first at something and can focus on deliberate integration of new technologies.

By focusing on creating meaningful experiences rather than announceables, public services can avoid hype cycles and ensure a more sustainable approach to adopting new technologies. This approach is particularly relevant for public services implementing new or greenfield technologies.

## Leadership health metrics

*133 words | Summarized in 2.9s*

Here are 3 concise paragraphs summarizing the article:

Team health metrics measure how well a team can deliver value for an organization and its users. However, these metrics do not account for the environment around a team, which is influenced by leadership performance.

Leadership health metrics aim to assess how effectively leaders create an environment that enables teams to perform at their best. Some potential indicators of good leadership include low staff churn, minimal reassignments per team member, and efficient decision-making processes.

Implementing these metrics would require accountability from leadership teams, but it can help identify areas for improvement and create a well-functioning environment for teams. The article invites readers to consider what they would measure, be proud to report on, and hold themselves accountable for solving in order to improve team performance.

## This idea of AI surpassing human ability is silly

*132 words | Summarized in 2.1s*

Here is a summary of the article in 2-3 concise paragraphs:

Jaron Lanier, a tech expert, disagrees with the idea that artificial intelligence (AI) will surpass human ability. He believes that comparing humans to AI is flawed, as AI is simply a tool that replicates human abilities rather than possessing them independently.

Lanier argues that this comparison is misleading, using the analogy of a car versus a human runner. Just as a car can be faster than a human runner, but not necessarily better at running, AI systems are limited to their programming and data, and cannot truly surpass human intelligence.

The implications of Lanier's views are that the focus should shift from fearing AI's potential to destroy us, to understanding how it drives us insane with its constant updates and changes.

The author believes that deadlines are rarely necessary, but can be useful in specific scenarios. A deadline is essential when strategic context requires it, such as getting to market first or following a key event for users.

Deadlines can help achieve outcomes for users by setting a healthy constraint for prioritization or scope management. In particular, they can be useful when launching a minimum viable product (MVP) or feature set, helping to think in iterations and deliver something valuable sooner.
